    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nAll experiments use Qwen3-32B unless noted; cross-model validation covers Dimensions~1--2, while secondary analyses (error taxonomy, DocRED $R^2$, TOST) remain single-model. Unless otherwise noted, all experiments use the v1 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}\n\\label{sec:count_curves}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}). The pattern is stable across three seeds (max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD; intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$). Count decline replicates on LLaMA-70B with comparable magnitude and on Gemini Flash (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_cross_model_gemini}). Count curves establish trend direction; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$) and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. Confusion rate is bidirectional $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by gold frequency sum; CIs are percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) excluded (per-instance RE format). Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs exclude zero (Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]).}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\citep{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. While pairwise $r^2$ is modest, correlated type pairs within batches compound this signal: batch-level BCS explains ${\\sim}$22\\% of RE accuracy variance on FewRel (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). The independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), which indicates that shared-representation bias is conservative. SCS outperforms simpler baselines (Levenshtein, WordNet Wu-Palmer, same-parent; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-baselines}). Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis} lists the most confusable type pairs with concrete examples.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition (Few-NERD NER and FewRel RE). IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS across all evaluated datasets ($\\rho = 0.84\\text{--}1.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} includes DocRED). RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nOn FewRel, BCS explains ${\\sim}$22\\% of cross-batch RE accuracy variance; confusability is a substantial predictor alongside count. We fix type count and vary BCS to isolate this effect. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound;\\footnote{Partial correlation controlling for degree centrality: FewRel $r_{\\text{partial}} = -0.537$ ($p < 0.001$, VIF${}\\,=\\,1.18$). DocRED partial correlation ($r_{\\text{partial}} = -0.348$, $p < 0.001$) is reported for completeness, but VIF${}\\,=\\,50.9$ (BCS--centrality $r = 0.99$) renders the estimate unstable; the clean evidence for BCS's independent contribution comes from FewRel (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_partial_corr}).} the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison on 100 random batches; based on a single extreme batch per BCS level at 16-rel scale; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (95\\% CI [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16) and confirms monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema ($n{=}55$ per count level;\\footnote{DocRED uses 5 batches per count level (vs.\\ 25 for FewRel) due to higher per-batch evaluation cost; CIs are correspondingly wider.}): extreme-BCS gap $-$25.0\\,pp at count 16 ($\\rho = -0.762$, 95\\% CI [$-$0.88, $-$0.58]) and $-$15.7\\,pp at count 32 ($\\rho = -0.693$, 95\\% CI [$-$0.83, $-$0.50]; both $p < 0.001$). Pooled across counts, $R^2 = 0.578$ ($n = 110$; 95\\% CI [0.46, 0.68]; per-count: $R^2 = 0.653$ at count 16, $0.614$ at count 32). The substantially higher $R^2$ relative to FewRel (0.218) largely reflects BCS--centrality collinearity (VIF${}\\,=\\,50.9$): semipartial $R^2_{\\text{BCS}} = 0.056$ after partialing degree centrality, confirming that 90\\% of the explained variance is shared with centrality. The clean evidence for BCS's independent contribution comes from FewRel (semipartial $R^2 = 0.273$, VIF${}\\,=\\,1.18$).\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\n\\looseness=-1\nDirect regression on 100 random 16-type batches from the 80-relation pool shows BCS explains 22\\% of cross-batch accuracy variance ($R^2 = 0.218$, $p < 0.001$). DocRED pooled $R^2 = 0.578$ ($n = 110$; semipartial $R^2_{\\text{BCS}} = 0.056$ after centrality; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_partial_corr}). The typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI [0.4, 4.0]). Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The amplification from pairwise $r^2 \\approx 1.5\\%$ to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ is not a statistical artifact but the operative mechanism: correlated confusable-type interactions compound within shared prompts \\citep{robinson-1950-ecological,baan-2023-ecological}, producing the batch-level signal that makes BCS practically predictive (${\\sim}3\\times$ amplification illustrated by Monte Carlo simulation; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\n\\looseness=-1\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: extreme-BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). All three open-weight families (Qwen3-32B, LLaMA-70B, LLaMA-8B) show consistent negative BCS slopes on FewRel RE. Gemini 2.5 Flash provides partial but fragile corroboration ($\\Delta = -17.6$\\,pp at count 8) with substantial leverage sensitivity (leave-one-out Pearson: $r = -0.80 \\to -0.42$, $p = 0.22$; Spearman: $\\rho = -0.40 \\to -0.18$, $p = 0.64$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_cross_model_gemini}). LLaMA-8B and LLaMA-70B both show no NER BCS signal ($r{=}{-}0.13$, $p{=}0.36$ for 70B), confirming the task asymmetry across three models (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_ner_null}).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\n\\looseness=-1\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp ($r = -0.919$, $p < 0.001$; $n{=}25$), modestly attenuated from v1 but highly significant. NER shows no batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under either prompt ($r \\approx 0$, $n = 450$; \\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}). The effect holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r \\leq -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), ruling out prompt design as the source. Few-shot demonstrations widen the confusability gap (+4.3\\,pp under 5-shot), though this finding is limited to a single dataset and count level (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_fewshot_bcs}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Dimensional Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS across all three datasets: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for Few-NERD NER, $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for FewRel sentence-level RE, and $\\rho = 0.843$ ($p < 10^{-30}$, $n = 110$) for DocRED document-level RE. The slightly lower DocRED correlation reflects additional noise from document-level context and cross-sentence reasoning. In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic; confusable types impair entity detection itself. In DocRED, IntraSub accounts for 72\\% of errors in high-BCS batches vs.\\ 15\\% in low-BCS batches; ExtraSub is zero (forced-choice format). Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches. Cross-model replication confirms the IntraSub--BCS correlation on FewRel RE: Gemini~2.5~Flash ($\\rho = 0.821$, $p < 0.001$, $n = 60$) and LLaMA-3.1-8B ($\\rho = 0.430$, $p = 0.001$, $n = 54$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_error_taxonomy}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThese results establish two dimensions: \\textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion). The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. We select the equivalence margin via convergent evidence: (i)~an interaction exceeding the main effect magnitude would undermine dimensional separability; (ii)~Cohen's $d = 0.5$ \\citep{cohen-1988-statistical} (medium effect) at the within-cell SD of 4.6\\,pp yields $\\pm 2.3$\\,pp. Both converge on $\\pm 2\\text{--}3$\\,pp.\\footnote{Margin sensitivity (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) confirms equivalence from $\\pm 2$\\,pp ($p = 0.047$) through $\\pm 5$\\,pp.} On the linear scale, TOST \\citep{lakens-2017-equivalence,schuirmann-1987-tost} bounds the FewRel interaction within $\\pm 3$\\,pp ($p = 0.003$); at $\\pm 2$\\,pp, equivalence is marginal ($p = 0.047$). Probability-scale TOST is the primary analysis because accuracy values (0.37--0.86) remain in the mid-range where logit and linear scales yield similar inference; the logit-scale result is inconclusive ($p = 0.209$; regression-based TOST: $p = 0.050$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}). On DocRED, the raw factorial interaction ($-$9.4\\,pp) is confounded by narrower BCS ranges at count 32; range-matched analysis reduces it to 0.93\\,pp, consistent with bounded interaction (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}). In NER, batch-level BCS signal is not detected at current power (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). Two factors explain the asymmetry: (1)~NER errors distribute across miss, boundary, and type modes rather than concentrating in type substitution (relaxed matching confirms this is not a metric artifact; Appendix~\\ref{app:relaxed-matching}), and (2)~BCS range restriction (SD ratio 19\\%) limits statistical detection power to 65\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_ner_power}); the factorial test is inconclusive ($p = 0.50$). At $n{=}450$ with BCS SD${}\\,=\\,0.027$, the minimum detectable correlation is $|r| \\approx 0.132$ ($\\alpha = 0.05$, power${}\\,=\\,0.80$), while FewRel's $r = -0.57$ would attenuate to $r \\approx -0.11$ under this SD compression. Current evidence cannot distinguish absence of a BCS$\\to$NER effect from an effect too small to detect---absence of evidence rather than evidence of absence. Post-hoc mitigation attempts---type renaming to reduce SCS and few-shot prompting---fail to close the confusability gap (Appendices~\\ref{app:renaming},~\\ref{sec:appendix_fewshot_bcs}), confirming that BCS captures a structural property of the schema--model interaction rather than a surface-level naming artifact.\n\n\\subsection{Centrality as a Potential Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation ($\\rho = +0.184\\text{--}0.208$, $p < 0.03$): FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly \\citep{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}, conflating confusability with centrality. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}), though the pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$). Few-NERD's cross-domain schema shows the expected negative direction ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$); mean BCS is reliable only for cross-domain schemas.",

          "+On FewRel, BCS explains ${\\sim}$22\\% of cross-batch RE accuracy variance; confusability is a substantial predictor alongside count. ```text
docs/paper/main.tex:328:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/main.tex:376:To verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition (Table~\ref{tab:prompt_variants}).
docs/paper/main.tex:404:We define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch to capture the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice (Table~\ref{tab:bcs_aggregation}).
docs/paper/main.tex:453:To test whether the BCS--accuracy relationship depends on the output format, we run both constrained (enum-based) and unconstrained (free-text) decoding on the same 25 batch type sets from the FewRel 80-relation pool (13 low-BCS, 12 high-BCS; count 8).
